# Daily Summary: 2025-06-10

### Prompting Science Report 2: The Decreasing Value of Chain of Thought in Prompting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07142v1)
- **Authors**: Lennart Meincke, Ethan Mollick, Lilach Mollick, Dan Shapiro
- **Abstract**: This is the second in a series of short reports that seek to help business, education, and policy leaders understand the technical details of working with AI through rigorous testing. In this report, we investigate Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting, a technique that encourages a large language model (LLM) to "think step by step" (Wei et al., 2022). CoT is a widely adopted method for improving reasoning tasks, however, our findings reveal a more nuanced picture of its effectiveness. We demonstrate two things: - The effectiveness of Chain-of-Thought prompting can vary greatly depending on the type of task and model. For non-reasoning models, CoT generally improves average performance by a small amount, particularly if the model does not inherently engage in step-by-step processing by default. However, CoT can introduce more variability in answers, sometimes triggering occasional errors in questions the model would otherwise get right. We also found that many recent models perform some form of CoT reasoning even if not asked; for these models, a request to perform CoT had little impact. Performing CoT generally requires far more tokens (increasing cost and time) than direct answers. - For models designed with explicit reasoning capabilities, CoT prompting often results in only marginal, if any, gains in answer accuracy. However, it significantly increases the time and tokens needed to generate a response.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting across different types of large language models (LLMs) on the GPQA Diamond dataset (PhD-level science questions).  It finds that the value of CoT prompting is decreasing. For non-reasoning models, CoT prompts can improve average performance slightly, but may also increase variability and error rates.  Crucially, the authors find that many recent models already perform CoT-like reasoning by default, making explicit CoT prompts less impactful.  For dedicated reasoning models, CoT prompting provides minimal gains in accuracy while significantly increasing processing time and token usage. The authors conclude that while CoT can be useful for older or smaller non-reasoning models, its benefits for dedicated reasoning models or newer models with implicit reasoning capabilities are questionable, especially considering the added computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper tackles an important and timely question: is CoT prompting still a valuable technique given the rapid advancements in LLM capabilities?  While the core concept (evaluating CoT) is not entirely new, the paper offers a nuanced and empirically supported perspective that challenges the widespread assumption that CoT is universally beneficial.  The paper's significance is amplified by the rigor of its experimental design, employing a challenging benchmark (GPQA), multiple models across different types, and a statistically sound evaluation methodology with reasonable repetitions.

Here's a breakdown of strengths and weaknesses affecting the novelty/significance score:

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a sound methodology, including the use of multiple trials and robust statistical comparisons.
*   **Relevant Benchmark:** GPQA Diamond provides a challenging and relatively unbiased testbed, as stated by its authors, for evaluating complex reasoning.
*   **Clear Contrasts:** The paper clearly contrasts CoT's impact on different model architectures (non-reasoning vs. reasoning models) and prompting styles (direct vs. step-by-step vs. default).
*   **Practical Implications:** The findings provide actionable insights for practitioners, suggesting when and when *not* to use CoT prompting to optimize performance and cost.
*   **Focus on Nuance:** The paper doesn't simply dismiss CoT.  It identifies specific contexts where it remains useful, adding value beyond a simplistic conclusion. It sheds light on the increasing cost associated with running the LLM's especially as models have a tendency to engage in step-by-step process even when not prompted.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Set:** While multiple models are used, the paper acknowledges the limitation of testing only a subset of available LLMs.  The specific models chosen, while representative, are not exhaustive. More extensive evaluation across a wider variety of LLMs could provide a greater perspective on the impact of CoT prompting.
*   **Simple CoT Prompts:** Although the effect of different prompts were explored, simple versions were used to keep the focus of the study on CoT vs Direct/Default. There may be room for further analysis of more advanced prompt engineering methods.
*   **Benchmark limitations:** The exclusive focus on a single benchmark, while well-justified, can somewhat limit the generalizability of the findings. While GPQA is intended to test higher-order reasoning abilities, it will be valuable to conduct additional analysis across a wider array of tasks.

**Overall Justification:**

The paper's strength lies in its rigorous empirical approach and its challenge to the often unquestioned adoption of CoT prompting. The findings are not revolutionary, but they are valuable. By highlighting the decreasing value of CoT and its conditional effectiveness, the paper promotes a more critical and informed use of this technique, leading to potential improvements in the design and implementation of reasoning prompts.

Score: 7

**Rationale:** The study offers practical insights into how models benefit from CoT prompting given the cost increase and potentially introducing a negative effect on the results. The rigorous approach and analysis justify the score. While not groundbreaking, the paper addresses a crucial issue with direct, actionable findings and contributes to a more nuanced understanding of prompting strategies. The model set is a little limited, the prompts were only simple in their design, and generalizability could be strengthened by evaluating a larger set of questions across a wider variety of benchmarks.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### GeometryZero: Improving Geometry Solving for LLM with Group Contrastive Policy Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07160v1)
- **Authors**: Yikun Wang, Yibin Wang, Dianyi Wang, Zimian Peng, Qipeng Guo, Dacheng Tao, Jiaqi Wang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across diverse domains, particularly in mathematical reasoning, amid which geometry problem solving remains a challenging area where auxiliary construction plays a enssential role. Existing approaches either achieve suboptimal performance or rely on massive LLMs (e.g., GPT-4o), incurring massive computational costs. We posit that reinforcement learning with verifiable reward (e.g., GRPO) offers a promising direction for training smaller models that effectively combine auxiliary construction with robust geometric reasoning. However, directly applying GRPO to geometric reasoning presents fundamental limitations due to its dependence on unconditional rewards, which leads to indiscriminate and counterproductive auxiliary constructions. To address these challenges, we propose Group Contrastive Policy Optimization (GCPO), a novel reinforcement learning framework featuring two key innovations: (1) Group Contrastive Masking, which adaptively provides positive or negative reward signals for auxiliary construction based on contextual utility, and a (2) length reward that promotes longer reasoning chains. Building on GCPO, we develop GeometryZero, a family of affordable-size geometric reasoning models that judiciously determine when to employ auxiliary construction. Our extensive empirical evaluation across popular geometric benchmarks (Geometry3K, MathVista) demonstrates that GeometryZero models consistently outperform baselines (e.g. GRPO), achieving an average improvement of 4.29% across all benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GeometryZero: Improving Geometry Solving for LLM with Group Contrastive Policy Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GeometryZero, a novel reinforcement learning framework designed to enhance the geometric reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). GeometryZero builds upon Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and addresses its limitations in handling auxiliary constructions (e.g., adding lines to diagrams). The core innovation lies in Group Contrastive Policy Optimization (GCPO), which incorporates two key elements: (1) *Group Contrastive Masking*, enabling adaptive positive or negative reward signals for auxiliary constructions based on contextual utility and (2) a *Length Reward* that encourages more extended reasoning chains. The authors demonstrate through experiments on popular geometric benchmarks (Geometry3K, MathVista) that GeometryZero consistently outperforms baseline models, including GRPO, in terms of overall performance and scaling effects across different model sizes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper introduces a seemingly incremental, yet significant, improvement to reinforcement learning for geometry problem-solving. GRPO already provides a solid foundation, but the GCPO additions are crucial for addressing a specific weakness: the indiscriminate use of auxiliary constructions. Group Contrastive Masking directly tackles this by providing conditional rewards, guiding the model to learn *when* to use these constructions. The *length reward*, though relatively simple, is another element that supports the overall goal by encouraging more in-depth reasoning. These two components constitute the main source of novelty.

**Significance:** Geometry problem-solving is a challenging area for LLMs, and improvements can have broader implications for visual reasoning and mathematical understanding. The use of auxiliary constructions is vital to unlocking the capabilities of LLMs to solve complex visual context problems. The authors focus on making smaller models achieve competitive results, which reduces computational costs when compared to using colossal LLMs and could make the method applicable for other more computationally limited tasks. By addressing the crucial issue of conditional auxiliary construction, GeometryZero contributes a valuable step toward more capable and efficient geometric reasoning systems. The empirical results demonstrate that GCPO is effective, outperforming existing methods and scaling well across model sizes. This helps validate the importance of conditional reasoning, especially in a domain like geometric problem-solving. The ablation studies further enhance the significance by demonstrating the contributions of each component. However, the reliance on having ground truth to verify the "accuracy reward" is a limitation. The framework might not be readily applicable in scenarios lacking this information. The paper provides enough justification that the problem tackled is crucial to advancing LLMs in problem solving.

**Strengths:**

*   Addresses a specific and important weakness in existing methods for geometric reasoning.
*   GCPO with both group contrastive masking and length reward leads to improvement and better outcomes with small size LLMs
*   Provides clear experimental results and ablation studies, demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach.
*   Offers detailed analyses and insights into the training dynamics and model behavior.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The GCPO approach relies on verifiable reward signals (i.e. ground truth), which may not be available for all geometric reasoning scenarios. This limits its applicability.
*   The improvement, while significant, is still fairly narrow and incremental.
*   Limited the experimentation to models under 7B parameters. This poses questions around how well the technique scales to other larger models.
*   A more detailed comparison to other prompting based methods for geometry construction would have been useful

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution that improves geometric reasoning in LLMs. The GCPO additions are novel and address a crucial limitation of existing approaches. The significance is evident in the improved performance and scaling effects, but the dependence on verifiable reward signals and limited scope of problems constrain the broader impact. It tackles a very specific problem using LLMs, so, while useful, it is a limited application.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### AMoPO: Adaptive Multi-objective Preference Optimization without Reward Models and Reference Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07165v1)
- **Authors**: Qi Liu, Jingqing Ruan, Hao Li, Haodong Zhao, Desheng Wang, Jiansong Chen, Wan Guanglu, Xunliang Cai, Zhi Zheng, Tong Xu
- **Abstract**: Existing multi-objective preference alignment methods for large language models (LLMs) face limitations: (1) the inability to effectively balance various preference dimensions, and (2) reliance on auxiliary reward/reference models introduces computational complexity. To address these challenges, we propose Adaptive Multi-objective Preference Optimization (AMoPO), a novel framework that achieves dynamic balance across preference dimensions. By introducing the multi-objective optimization paradigm to use the dimension-aware generation metrics as implicit rewards, AMoPO aligns LLMs with diverse preferences without additional reward models or reference models. We introduce an adaptive weight assignment mechanism that models the generation space as a Gaussian distribution, allowing dynamic prioritization of preference dimensions. Empirical results demonstrate that AMoPO outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by 28.5%, and the experiments on 7B, 14B, and 32B models reveal the scaling ability of AMoPO. Moreover, additional analysis of multiple dimensions verifies its adaptability and effectiveness. These findings validate AMoPO's capability to achieve dimension-aware preference alignment, highlighting its superiority. Our codes and datasets are available at https://github.com/Javkonline/AMoPO.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary of the paper "AMOPO: Adaptive Multi-objective Preference Optimization without Reward Models and Reference Models," along with a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AMOPO (Adaptive Multi-objective Preference Optimization), a novel framework for aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with multi-dimensional human preferences. Unlike existing methods that rely on auxiliary reward models or reference models, AMOPO uses dimension-aware generation metrics as implicit rewards, thereby simplifying the alignment process.  It also introduces an adaptive weight assignment mechanism that dynamically prioritizes preference dimensions based on a Gaussian distribution model of the generation space. Empirical results demonstrate that AMOPO outperforms state-of-the-art baselines on several benchmarks, exhibiting superior performance and scaling abilities across different model sizes. The paper emphasizes the adaptability and effectiveness of AMOPO in achieving dimension-aware preference alignment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Approach:** AMOPO offers a genuinely novel approach by eliminating the need for auxiliary reward or reference models. This simplifies the training pipeline and reduces computational complexity, which is a significant advantage.
    *   **Adaptive Weighting:** The adaptive weight assignment mechanism provides a sophisticated way to balance multiple preference dimensions dynamically. This is a crucial improvement over methods using fixed weights, as it allows the LLM to adapt to varying contexts and user needs.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents compelling empirical evidence demonstrating that AMOPO outperforms existing methods on various benchmarks. This showcases its effectiveness in practice.
    *   **Scaling Ability:** The experiments across different model sizes (7B, 14B, and 32B) confirm the scalability of AMOPO, indicating its potential for use with larger and more powerful LLMs.
    *   **Focus on a Real-World Problem:** Multi-objective alignment is a significant challenge for the safe and controllable deployment of AI systems. Addressing this problem with a practical and efficient solution is highly relevant.
    *   **Open Source Code and Datasets:**  Providing code and datasets makes the research more accessible and facilitates further experimentation and validation by the community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset limitations:** While HelpSteer2 is used to train the policy model on, the paper lacks consideration for the impact of different training datasets, such as Ultrafeedback.

    *   **Multi-Turn Dialogue focus:** While AMOPO handles static preference scenarios effectively, further work is needed to manage dynamic preference changes that occur during ongoing interactions.

    *   **Dimension diversity:** in our experiment, we consider three dimensions; that means the Gaussian distribution assumption for modelling the generation space may not generalize well to all types of preference dimensions.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Theoretical contribution:** Introduces an objective function combining a Multi-Objective Bradley-Terry Model, an adaptive-weight policy and Multi-Objective Optimization, reducing auxiliary models such as reward and reference models.

    *   **Practical Impact:** AMOPO has the potential to significantly reduce the computational cost and complexity of aligning LLMs with human preferences, making it more accessible to researchers and practitioners with limited resources.
    *   **Influence on the Field:** The idea of using implicit rewards derived from generation metrics could inspire new directions in preference alignment research, moving away from reliance on external reward models. The adaptive weighting mechanism offers a robust solution to balancing conflicting preferences in LLMs, which will likely be adopted and extended by other researchers.

*   **Novelty:** The work is novel due to its unique use of Multi-Objective Optimization and the Adaptive Weight Policy with an implicit rewards method. It also differs from other alignment methods.

**Justification for Score:**

AMOPO presents a significant advancement in multi-objective preference alignment. Its novelty lies in the elimination of explicit reward/reference models and the introduction of an adaptive weighting scheme. The empirical results demonstrate its effectiveness, and the scaling experiments further validate its potential. I recognize the limitations regarding datasets and dimension diversity. Considering these strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **8.5**.

Score: 8.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Efficient Text-Attributed Graph Learning through Selective Annotation and Graph Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07168v1)
- **Authors**: Huanyi Xie, Lijie Hu, Lu Yu, Tianhao Huang, Longfei Li, Meng Li, Jun Zhou, Huan Wang, Di Wang
- **Abstract**: In the realm of Text-attributed Graphs (TAGs), traditional graph neural networks (GNNs) often fall short due to the complex textual information associated with each node. Recent methods have improved node representations by leveraging large language models (LLMs) to enhance node text features, but these approaches typically require extensive annotations or fine-tuning across all nodes, which is both time-consuming and costly. To overcome these challenges, we introduce GAGA, an efficient framework for TAG representation learning. GAGA reduces annotation time and cost by focusing on annotating only representative nodes and edges. It constructs an annotation graph that captures the topological relationships among these annotations. Furthermore, GAGA employs a two-level alignment module to effectively integrate the annotation graph with the TAG, aligning their underlying structures. Experiments show that GAGA achieves classification accuracies on par with or surpassing state-of-the-art methods while requiring only 1% of the data to be annotated, demonstrating its high efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Efficient Text-Attributed Graph Learning through Selective Annotation and Graph Alignment":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GAGA, a novel framework for efficient representation learning on Text-Attributed Graphs (TAGs). GAGA addresses the computational cost and time associated with using Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance node representations in TAGs.  Instead of annotating all nodes (or edges), GAGA selectively annotates only representative nodes/edges using LLMs. It then constructs an annotation graph from these annotations and uses a two-level contrastive learning approach to align the annotation graph with the original TAG. This alignment efficiently integrates semantic information from LLMs with topological information from Graph Neural Networks (GNNs). The framework fine-tunes only the GNN component. The authors demonstrate through experiments on several datasets that GAGA achieves comparable or superior performance to state-of-the-art methods while requiring significantly less annotation effort (1% of the data), thus improving efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper offers a novel approach to TAG learning by combining selective annotation with a two-level graph alignment strategy. This addresses a significant practical limitation of previous methods that relied on annotating or fine-tuning over the entire dataset which can be cost prohibitive. The idea of using LLMs for annotation and then aligning the resulting graph with the original structure is a valuable contribution.  The two-level contrastive learning aspect (subgraph and prototype level) offers a potentially generalizable technique.

**Significance:**  The significance lies in improving the scalability and efficiency of TAG learning. By reducing the annotation requirements drastically, the method makes LLM-enhanced TAG analysis accessible to larger and more complex datasets, which is crucial in real-world applications. The empirical results validate the efficiency gains. The framework also explores explanation-guided learning by framing annotations from LLMs as explanations which can guide contrastive learning for improved performance. The significance is further strengthened by the comprehensive experiments across a diverse set of datasets and comparisons to strong baselines. However, the limitations section acknowledges that GAGA is not directly applicable to new TAGs without retraining. While not severely limiting, it would be more useful as a pre-trained model to directly apply to different graph datasets.

**Strengths:**

*   **Efficiency:** The primary strength is the significant reduction in annotation cost and time, making LLM-enhanced TAG learning practical.
*   **Performance:**  GAGA achieves competitive or superior performance compared to state-of-the-art methods on node classification and link prediction tasks.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents thorough experiments across multiple datasets and compares against a range of strong baselines.
*   **Clear Methodology:**  The approach is well-explained and easy to follow, with a clear description of the GAGA framework and its components.
*   **Addresses a Practical Problem:**  The paper directly tackles a crucial bottleneck in applying LLMs to graph learning, enhancing its practicality.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Specific Alignment:** A major limitation as noted by the authors themselves is that the proposed method requires dataset specific alignment and cannot be directly applied to new TAGs.
*   **Dependence on Representative Node Selection:** The performance hinges on the accuracy of representative node selection. The information density metric might not always capture the most informative nodes, especially in more complex graphs. The impact of different selection strategies could be further explored.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The two-level alignment process introduces several hyperparameters (e.g., *α*, number of neighbors, prototype dimension). The paper provides some guidance, but a more extensive analysis of their sensitivity would be beneficial.
*   **No Direct Comparison of Different Prompts:** Experiments could benefit from exploring different types of prompts for annotation in addition to providing prompts. The paper mentions the use of prompts for downstream classification and edge explanation, but the quality of prompts affect the quality of the annotation graph.

**Potential Influence:**

GAGA has the potential to influence research in TAG learning by providing a more efficient and scalable approach. It could inspire further work on selective annotation strategies, graph alignment techniques, and the integration of LLMs with GNNs. It opens opportunities for applying these methods to larger and more complex real-world graphs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

GAGA introduces a significant efficiency improvement and exhibits strong performance. The novel graph alignment strategy coupled with selective annotation presents a clear advancement in the field. The comprehensive experimental validation across various datasets bolsters its credibility. However, the dependence on dataset specific alignment, potential sensitivity to hyperparameters, dependence on quality of prompts, and limited exploration of alternative node selection strategies prevents it from achieving a higher score. It is a solid and practical contribution, but with some limitations in generalizability, further research is needed to address these areas and increase the broader applicability of the approach.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### CTDGSI: A comprehensive exploitation of instance selection methods for automatic text classification. VII Concurso de Teses, Dissertações e Trabalhos de Graduação em SI -- XXI Simpósio Brasileiro de Sistemas de Informação
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07169v1)
- **Authors**: Washington Cunha, Leonardo Rocha, Marcos André Gonçalves
- **Abstract**: Progress in Natural Language Processing (NLP) has been dictated by the rule of more: more data, more computing power and more complexity, best exemplified by the Large Language Models. However, training (or fine-tuning) large dense models for specific applications usually requires significant amounts of computing resources. This \textbf{Ph.D. dissertation} focuses on an under-investi\-gated NLP data engineering technique, whose potential is enormous in the current scenario known as Instance Selection (IS). The IS goal is to reduce the training set size by removing noisy or redundant instances while maintaining the effectiveness of the trained models and reducing the training process cost. We provide a comprehensive and scientifically sound comparison of IS methods applied to an essential NLP task -- Automatic Text Classification (ATC), considering several classification solutions and many datasets. Our findings reveal a significant untapped potential for IS solutions. We also propose two novel IS solutions that are noise-oriented and redundancy-aware, specifically designed for large datasets and transformer architectures. Our final solution achieved an average reduction of 41\% in training sets, while maintaining the same levels of effectiveness in all datasets. Importantly, our solutions demonstrated speedup improvements of 1.67x (up to 2.46x), making them scalable for datasets with hundreds of thousands of documents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided dissertation abstract:

**Summary:**

The dissertation focuses on Instance Selection (IS) techniques within Natural Language Processing (NLP), specifically for Automatic Text Classification (ATC).  It investigates the potential of IS to reduce training set sizes by removing noisy or redundant instances, thereby reducing training costs while maintaining model effectiveness, particularly relevant in the context of resource-intensive large language models (LLMs). The work compares several IS methods, proposes two novel IS solutions (one redundancy-oriented and one noise-oriented & redundancy-aware), and evaluates them against various classification models and datasets. The final proposed solution achieves significant training set reduction and speedups while maintaining effectiveness. The work culminated in several publications, including top-tier Information Systems journals.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strength:**  The core novelty lies in the comprehensive investigation of IS methods *specifically* for NLP, and even more specifically for ATC, a relatively unexplored area. The creation of two novel IS algorithms, E2SC and a bi-objective variant (biO-IS), that are designed for modern, large datasets and transformer architectures contributes significantly to the knowledge base. The creation of new taxonomy is another source of novelty.
*   **Weakness:** While IS itself isn't a brand-new area, the focus on *transformer-based* ATC and dealing with real-world large and skewed datasets is where novelty resides. The use of KNN as part of the E2SC for the "calibrated" redundancy estimate is a potential weakness; the Dissertation will need to rigorously justify that choice (which the text implies is explored).  The dependence on TF-IDF initially might also limit some of its applicability to scenarios where embedding-based approaches are more advantageous from an effectiveness perspective.

**Significance:**

*   **Strength:** The increasing demand for computational resources in NLP, particularly with LLMs, makes this work extremely significant. Reducing training costs without sacrificing model performance is a crucial area of research. The demonstrated speedups and reduction in data size have practical implications for researchers and practitioners. The publication record in top-tier journals supports the quality and significance of the work. Addressing the "sustainability" of NLP through more efficient training techniques is timely and important.
*   **Weakness:** The significance hinges on the generalizability of the results.  While tested on a variety of datasets, the scope of ATC might limit its applicability to other NLP tasks. The dissertation needs to provide compelling arguments for why the proposed IS methods are suitable for a wider range of NLP problems. The analysis is centered around a fixed set of hyperparameters, and it may be hard to extend the results to situations outside these restrictions.

**Rigour & Justification:**

The work appears rigorous, evidenced by the extensive experimentation (4000+ experiments) and the use of statistical tests (paired t-test, Bonferroni correction). The claims are backed by empirical results across a wide range of datasets.

**Detailed Justification for the Score:**

The dissertation tackles a critical and timely problem in NLP, exhibiting novelty in its focused application of IS techniques to transformer-based ATC and the design of new IS algorithms tailored for this domain. The significance stems from the potential to reduce computational costs and make NLP more sustainable. The rigorous methodology and strong publication record further strengthen its value. It can be argued that the innovation lies in the application and tailoring of existing concepts for a specific niche (IS methods for transformers in ATC) rather than revolutionary algorithmic breakthroughs. Therefore a perfect score is not warranted.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### RULE: Reinforcement UnLEarning Achieves Forget-Retain Pareto Optimality
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07171v1)
- **Authors**: Chenlong Zhang, Zhuoran Jin, Hongbang Yuan, Jiaheng Wei, Tong Zhou, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao, Yubo Chen
- **Abstract**: The widespread deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs) trained on massive, uncurated corpora has raised growing concerns about the inclusion of sensitive, copyrighted, or illegal content. This has led to increasing interest in LLM unlearning: the task of selectively removing specific information from a model without retraining from scratch or degrading overall utility. However, existing methods often rely on large-scale forget and retain datasets, and suffer from unnatural responses, poor generalization, or catastrophic utility loss. In this work, we propose Reinforcement UnLearning (RULE), an efficient framework that formulates unlearning as a refusal boundary optimization problem. RULE is trained with a small portion of the forget set and synthesized boundary queries, using a verifiable reward function that encourages safe refusal on forget--related queries while preserving helpful responses on permissible inputs. We provide both theoretical and empirical evidence demonstrating the effectiveness of RULE in achieving targeted unlearning without compromising model utility. Experimental results show that, with only $12%$ forget set and $8%$ synthesized boundary data, RULE outperforms existing baselines by up to $17.5%$ forget quality and $16.3%$ naturalness response while maintaining general utility, achieving forget--retain Pareto optimality. Remarkably, we further observe that RULE improves the naturalness of model outputs, enhances training efficiency, and exhibits strong generalization ability, generalizing refusal behavior to semantically related but unseen queries.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Reinforcement UnLEarning (RULE), a novel framework for selectively removing sensitive information from Large Language Models (LLMs) without sacrificing overall utility.  RULE formulates unlearning as a refusal boundary optimization problem, training the model to refuse forget-related queries while maintaining helpful responses to permissible inputs. This is achieved through a two-stage process: first, rejection steering guides the model to refuse queries in the forget set, and second, reinforcement learning optimizes the refusal boundary using a small portion of the forget set and synthesized boundary queries. The authors demonstrate both theoretically and empirically that RULE achieves targeted unlearning while preserving model utility.  Experiments show that RULE outperforms existing baselines in forget quality, naturalness of responses, and data efficiency, achieving a better forget-retain Pareto optimality. The method is also shown to improve training efficiency, response naturalness, and generalize well to semantically related unseen queries.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper demonstrates novelty on several fronts:

    *   **Refusal Boundary Formulation:** Formulating unlearning as a reinforcement learning problem focused on optimizing a refusal boundary is a distinct approach compared to existing methods that primarily rely on gradient manipulation or preference optimization.  This allows for more targeted control over the model's behavior.
    *   **Data Efficiency:**  The method's ability to achieve strong results with a small fraction of the forget set (12%) and boundary data (8%) is a significant advantage compared to approaches requiring large-scale forget and retain datasets. This makes it more practical for real-world scenarios.
    *   **Emphasis on Response Naturalness:**  The paper explicitly addresses and evaluates the naturalness of model responses after unlearning.  This is a valuable contribution, as many existing methods produce unnatural or evasive responses, which can be a security risk and degrade user experience.
    *   **Generalization to Unseen Queries:** The ability to generalize refusal behavior to semantically related but unseen queries is a critical feature, demonstrating that the model learns a deeper understanding of what to refuse rather than just memorizing specific queries.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Practical Impact:**  The data efficiency and improved naturalness of RULE make it more likely to be adopted in real-world applications where resources are limited and user experience is paramount. The ability to handle unseen related queries expands the range of scenarios where this unlearning method can be effectively deployed.
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** Providing theoretical bounds helps to ground the proposed approach, offering guarantees that RULE reduces the mis-refusal risk under specific conditions.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper includes a thorough experimental evaluation on multiple benchmarks (RWKU and MUSE), comparing RULE against several strong baselines. The use of multiple metrics, including forget quality, retain quality, and response naturalness, provides a comprehensive assessment of the method's performance.
    *   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** The inclusion of ablation studies helps demonstrate the impact of each of the components that are included within the method, such as the rejection steering stage, and the source of the samples that are included.
*   **Weaknesses and Limitations:**

    *   **Reliance on Synthetic Boundary Data:** The method relies on synthetic boundary data generated by GPT-4o, which might introduce biases or limitations depending on the quality of the generator. While the paper demonstrates good performance with this approach, exploring alternative ways to obtain or generate boundary data would be a valuable avenue for future research.
    *   **Complexity:** RL-based approaches are typically more complex to implement and tune than simple gradient based methods. Although, the benefits are made clear.
    *   **Scalability to Larger Models & Datasets:** While the paper demonstrates effectiveness on llama3-8b, it would be beneficial to see how RULE scales to larger LLMs and more complex unlearning scenarios.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Boundary Discovery:** The paper depends upon a manually constructed boundary with entity substitutions. While this boundary is shown to be sufficient, the ability to learn boundaries directly from the raw data of a large language model is a compelling avenue for future investigation.
*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper's emphasis on refusal behavior as a key learning signal could inspire new directions in LLM unlearning research.
    *   The data-efficient approach could make LLM unlearning more accessible to organizations with limited resources.
    *   The focus on response naturalness could lead to the development of more user-friendly and trustworthy unlearning systems.

**Justification for the Score:**

RULE offers a combination of novelty, practical significance, and comprehensive evaluation that makes it a substantial contribution to the field of LLM unlearning.  The formulation as a refusal boundary optimization problem, combined with its data efficiency and emphasis on response naturalness, addresses key limitations of existing methods.  The potential influence of this work is considerable, as it offers a more practical and user-friendly approach to selectively removing sensitive information from LLMs. With that said, there are limitations that future research should address such as the reliance on synthetic boundary data and scalability to larger models/datasets.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Frame Guidance: Training-Free Guidance for Frame-Level Control in Video Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07177v1)
- **Authors**: Sangwon Jang, Taekyung Ki, Jaehyeong Jo, Jaehong Yoon, Soo Ye Kim, Zhe Lin, Sung Ju Hwang
- **Abstract**: Advancements in diffusion models have significantly improved video quality, directing attention to fine-grained controllability. However, many existing methods depend on fine-tuning large-scale video models for specific tasks, which becomes increasingly impractical as model sizes continue to grow. In this work, we present Frame Guidance, a training-free guidance for controllable video generation based on frame-level signals, such as keyframes, style reference images, sketches, or depth maps. For practical training-free guidance, we propose a simple latent processing method that dramatically reduces memory usage, and apply a novel latent optimization strategy designed for globally coherent video generation. Frame Guidance enables effective control across diverse tasks, including keyframe guidance, stylization, and looping, without any training, compatible with any video models. Experimental results show that Frame Guidance can produce high-quality controlled videos for a wide range of tasks and input signals.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Frame Guidance: Training-Free Guidance for Frame-Level Control in Video Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Frame Guidance," a novel training-free method for controlling video generation using pre-trained video diffusion models (VDMs). Frame Guidance allows users to guide the generation process through frame-level signals such as keyframes, style reference images, sketches, or depth maps. The key innovations are: 1) "Latent slicing," which efficiently computes guidance losses by decoding only relevant temporal slices of the video latent space, significantly reducing memory requirements. 2) "Video Latent Optimization" (VLO), a latent optimization strategy that prioritizes a deterministic update of the latent space during early inference steps to establish a globally coherent video layout, then switches to a stochastic update strategy to refine the details.  The method is model-agnostic and demonstrated on various tasks, including keyframe-guided generation, stylization, and looped video generation, using different VDMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper exhibits good novelty.  While training-free control for image generation is relatively well-explored, extending this concept effectively to video generation presents distinct challenges, mainly due to the computational complexity and temporal coherence requirements. Latent slicing is a valuable practical contribution that addresses the high memory demands associated with CausalVAE-based video diffusion models by exploiting temporal locality. VLO, the two-stage latent optimization approach (deterministic for layout, stochastic for details), appears to be an effective way to balance global coherence and local detail, and address limitations of straight time travel.

**Significance:** The significance of the paper is substantial.  The training-free nature of Frame Guidance allows it to be readily applied to new and existing VDMs without requiring costly fine-tuning. Its ability to handle diverse frame-level inputs (keyframes, style images, depth maps, sketches) makes it a flexible and general-purpose control method. This increases accessibility for users who lack the resources to fine-tune large video models.  The demonstrated results highlight the capability to generate high-quality, controllable videos.

**Strengths:**

*   **Training-free and Model-Agnostic:**  The method is readily deployable on various VDMs without the need for retraining.
*   **Memory Efficiency:** The latent slicing technique reduces memory requirements significantly, enabling guidance on resource-constrained hardware.
*   **Generality:** The framework is not limited to a specific task or input type, supporting a wide range of control signals and applications.
*   **Effective Optimization Strategy:** VLO addresses the challenges of maintaining temporal coherence while allowing for detailed refinement.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper provides convincing qualitative and quantitative evidence demonstrating the effectiveness of Frame Guidance across multiple tasks and models.  Human evaluations show noticeable improvements.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** While training-free, guidance sampling still requires backpropagation and multiple model predictions, which increases inference time (2-4x) compared to the base model. Although they reduce memory requirements well, the method still requires significant computational resources.
*   **Dependence on Base Model:** The method's performance is limited by the capabilities of the underlying video diffusion model. This means it might struggle with highly dynamic scenes or objects with very fine details if the pre-trained model doesn't represent these well.
*   **Lack of comparison with other "general" training free controllable video methods:** In the related works section, the authors mention several methods with similar control aims. There is no detailed discussion on the differences between their method and those methods. The authors also do not provide any comparissons.
*   **Limited Analysis:** The explanation of why CausalVAE has temporal locality is not sufficient and could be expanded on.

**Potential Influence:**

Frame Guidance has the potential to be widely adopted as a practical and flexible control method for video generation. The simplicity and generality of the framework make it attractive for both researchers and practitioners. The insights gained from latent slicing and VLO can also inform the design of future controllable video generation methods. While a user-friendly implementation is not shown, the approach has a strong potential to significantly simplify the process of creating controlled videos, broadening access for creative and professional use cases.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the good novelty, significant contribution to practical controllable video generation, and solid experimental validation, but keeping in mind the computational cost and reliance on base model capabilities, a score of **7.5** is appropriate.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Flattery in Motion: Benchmarking and Analyzing Sycophancy in Video-LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07180v1)
- **Authors**: Wenrui Zhou, Shu Yang, Qingsong Yang, Zikun Guo, Lijie Hu, Di Wang
- **Abstract**: As video large language models (Video-LLMs) become increasingly integrated into real-world applications that demand grounded multimodal reasoning, ensuring their factual consistency and reliability is of critical importance. However, sycophancy, the tendency of these models to align with user input even when it contradicts the visual evidence, undermines their trustworthiness in such contexts. Current sycophancy research has largely overlooked its specific manifestations in the video-language domain, resulting in a notable absence of systematic benchmarks and targeted evaluations to understand how Video-LLMs respond under misleading user input. To fill this gap, we propose VISE (Video-LLM Sycophancy Benchmarking and Evaluation), the first dedicated benchmark designed to evaluate sycophantic behavior in state-of-the-art Video-LLMs across diverse question formats, prompt biases, and visual reasoning tasks. Specifically, VISE pioneeringly brings linguistic perspectives on sycophancy into the visual domain, enabling fine-grained analysis across multiple sycophancy types and interaction patterns. In addition, we explore key-frame selection as an interpretable, training-free mitigation strategy, which reveals potential paths for reducing sycophantic bias by strengthening visual grounding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VISE, a new benchmark designed to evaluate sycophancy (the tendency to align with user input even when it contradicts evidence) in Video Large Language Models (Video-LLMs).  VISE includes a diverse set of videos and questions, categorized by sycophancy type, prompt bias, and visual reasoning tasks. The authors evaluate several state-of-the-art Video-LLMs using VISE, identifying common failure cases and the impact of model scale and question structure on sycophantic behavior. They also propose and evaluate a key-frame selection strategy as a training-free method to mitigate sycophancy by enhancing visual grounding.  The analysis includes an investigation into how key-frame selection affects internal model attention dynamics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a significant gap in the literature.  While sycophancy has been studied in text-based LLMs, its manifestation and analysis in the more complex video-language domain is relatively unexplored. Introducing VISE is a concrete and valuable contribution, providing a standardized way to assess this issue. Adapting existing linguistic notions of sycophancy to the video domain and accounting for temporal dynamics represents a genuine advance. The exploration of different sycophancy types and prompt structures, specifically within the context of videos, adds further novelty. Key-frame selection as a mitigation strategy is reasonably innovative.

*   **Significance:**  The problem of sycophancy is crucial for the reliable deployment of Video-LLMs in real-world applications.  Ensuring factual consistency and visual grounding is essential for trustworthiness.  By providing a benchmark and analysis of the problem, the paper directly addresses this need and helps to prioritize future research directions. The results highlighting vulnerabilities in current Video-LLMs, especially in areas like temporal reasoning and with certain prompt biases, are valuable for guiding model development and safety considerations. The demonstration of a mitigation strategy, even a relatively simple one, is significant, suggesting possible paths toward more reliable models. The attention analysis provides valuable insight into why key-frame selection is effective, improving model understanding.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed benchmark with diverse scenarios.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of multiple state-of-the-art models.
    *   Analysis of contributing factors and types of sycophancy.
    *   Proposal and evaluation of a practical mitigation strategy.
    *   Investigation of model attention dynamics to understand the mitigation strategy.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The key-frame selection mitigation strategy, while effective, is relatively simple. More sophisticated approaches might yield even greater improvements.
    *   While the paper tests a variety of Video-LLMs, a wider selection would further strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The reliance on multiple-choice questions, while practical, may limit the models' expressiveness and ability to demonstrate nuanced understanding or disagreement.
    *   The analysis of attention shifts focuses on a representative Video-LLM (InternVL-2.5), results may not generalize to other architectures.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to stimulate further research in this area.  VISE provides a concrete tool for researchers to evaluate their models and compare their performance. The findings and analysis of attention shifts could inform the development of new training techniques and model architectures that are less susceptible to sycophancy. The key-frame selection mitigation provides a baseline for further improvement of visually-grounded video reasoning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper introduces a novel and relevant benchmark to a previously underexplored area.  The analysis is comprehensive and provides practical insights into the problem of sycophancy in Video-LLMs, and contributes a simple, but promising, mitigation technique. While there are some limitations, they do not significantly diminish the value of the paper.
Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mitigating Behavioral Hallucination in Multimodal Large Language Models for Sequential Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07184v1)
- **Authors**: Liangliang You, Junchi Yao, Shu Yang, Guimin Hu, Lijie Hu, Di Wang
- **Abstract**: While multimodal large language models excel at various tasks, they still suffer from hallucinations, which limit their reliability and scalability for broader domain applications. To address this issue, recent research mainly focuses on objective hallucination. However, for sequential images, besides objective hallucination, there is also behavioral hallucination, which is less studied. This work aims to fill in the gap. We first reveal that behavioral hallucinations mainly arise from two key factors: prior-driven bias and the snowball effect. Based on these observations, we introduce SHE (Sequence Hallucination Eradication), a lightweight, two-stage framework that (1) detects hallucinations via visual-textual alignment check using our proposed adaptive temporal window and (2) mitigates them via orthogonal projection onto the joint embedding space. We also propose a new metric (BEACH) to quantify behavioral hallucination severity. Empirical results on standard benchmarks demonstrate that SHE reduces behavioral hallucination by over 10% on BEACH while maintaining descriptive accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of behavioral hallucination in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) when dealing with sequential images.  The authors argue that existing methods primarily focus on *object* hallucinations in single images, neglecting the temporal inconsistencies and implausible actions (behavioral hallucinations) that arise in image sequences. They identify two key factors causing these hallucinations: prior-driven bias and the snowball effect (where initial errors compound over time).  To mitigate this, they propose SHE (Sequence Hallucination Eradication), a two-stage framework. First, SHE detects hallucinations using a visual-textual alignment check with an adaptive temporal window. Second, it mitigates hallucinations via orthogonal projection in the joint embedding space.  The paper also introduces a new evaluation metric, BEACH, to specifically quantify the severity of behavioral hallucinations. Experimental results on standard benchmarks are presented to demonstrate that SHE reduces behavioral hallucinations while maintaining descriptive accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in explicitly addressing *behavioral* hallucinations in sequential images, a problem less explored compared to object hallucinations.  The identification of the "snowball effect" as a contributing factor is also a noteworthy insight. The two-stage SHE framework and the BEACH metric offer concrete solutions and a more focused evaluation approach. The Adaptive Temporal Windowing provides a novel technique to handle sequential errors.

*   **Significance:** The problem of hallucinations in MLLMs is a significant bottleneck limiting their deployment in real-world applications, especially in sensitive domains.  By focusing on behavioral hallucinations, the paper tackles a critical limitation for applications involving video understanding, robotic control, and interactive visual narratives. The proposed SHE framework's lightweight nature (avoiding retraining) could make it practical for integration with existing MLLMs. The BEACH metric provides a valuable tool for evaluating and comparing different hallucination mitigation techniques specific to behavior.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of behavioral hallucination and differentiates it from object hallucination.
    *   **Causal Analysis:**  The analysis of prior-driven bias and the snowball effect provides valuable insight into the causes of the problem.
    *   **Methodology:** The SHE framework offers a well-defined, implementable solution. The adaptive temporal windowing is an interesting addition.
    *   **Evaluation:**  The introduction of the BEACH metric is a significant contribution, as it provides a more targeted way to evaluate behavioral hallucination mitigation. The experiments are conducted on multiple datasets and models.
    *   **Lightweight:** SHE does not require retraining or fine-tuning of the original model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Generalizability:** While the paper shows promising results on the tested benchmarks, the generalizability of SHE to more complex or noisy datasets is not fully established.  Applications to video, for example, may require further adaptation.
    *   **Threshold Sensitivity:**  The performance of SHE likely depends on the sensitivity of threshold-based decisions in the detection and mitigation steps. The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of how these thresholds are determined and their impact on performance.
    *   **Dependency on Internal Representations:** A significant limitation is the reliance on accessing intermediate representations of the MLLM.  This may not be possible for proprietary models with closed architectures, limiting the framework's applicability in some scenarios.
    *   **Limited comparison to other behavioral mitigation works:** While the paper references Volcano and Self-PEP, it could benefit from a more in-depth comparison, highlighting specific advantages of SHE in terms of performance, efficiency, or robustness.

*   **Potential Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research on hallucination mitigation in MLLMs, particularly for applications involving sequential data. The BEACH metric could become a standard evaluation tool for behavioral hallucination.  The SHE framework provides a practical starting point for developing more robust and reliable MLLMs.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper tackles a significant and relatively underexplored problem with a well-defined methodology and a new evaluation metric. The identification of the snowball effect and the adaptive temporal windowing technique are valuable contributions. However, the dependency on accessing internal representations and the limited comparison to prior work on behavioral hallucination prevention slightly limit its impact.  The generalizability of SHE needs further investigation. Overall, the paper makes a solid contribution to the field of trustworthy AI and deserves a strong positive evaluation.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring Effective Strategies for Building a Customised GPT Agent for Coding Classroom Dialogues
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07194v1)
- **Authors**: Luwei Bai, Dongkeun Han, Sara Hennessy
- **Abstract**: This study investigates effective strategies for developing a customised GPT agent to code classroom dialogue. While classroom dialogue is widely recognised as a crucial element of education, its analysis remains challenging due to the need for a nuanced understanding of dialogic functions and the labour-intensive nature of manual transcript coding. Recent advancements in large language models offer promising avenues for automating this process. However, existing studies predominantly focus on training large-scale models or evaluating pre-trained models with fixed codebooks, which are often not applicable or replicable for dialogue researchers working with small datasets or customised coding schemes. Using GPT-4's MyGPT agent as a case, this study evaluates its baseline performance in coding classroom dialogue with a human codebook and examines how performance varies with different example inputs through a variable control method. Through a design-based research approach, it identifies a set of practical strategies, based on MyGPT's unique features, for configuring effective agents with limited data. The findings suggest that, despite some limitations, a MyGPT agent developed with these strategies can serve as a useful coding assistant by generating coding suggestions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates effective strategies for building a customized GPT agent (specifically using OpenAI's MyGPT agent) for coding classroom dialogues.  It addresses the challenges of manual coding in dialogue analysis, the limitations of existing automated approaches, and the need for accessible and replicable solutions for researchers with limited data and resources.  The study uses a design-based research approach, testing different configurations of the MyGPT agent (varying example input, structured instructions, etc.) and evaluating its performance using the Cambridge Dialogue Analysis Scheme (CDAS). The results highlight the importance of structured instructions, cognitive load management, and iterative refinement in building an effective coding assistant with limited data. The paper proposes a set of practical strategies for configuring these agents and reports on an agent that is approaching the performance of human coders, as assessed by inter-rater agreement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and practical problem: automating dialogue analysis in educational research. The manual coding process is labor-intensive and prone to biases. LLMs offer promise, but existing solutions often require extensive data or specialized expertise, which is a barrier to entry for many researchers.

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Focus:** The study focuses on a readily available tool (MyGPT) and seeks to develop strategies applicable to researchers with limited data and resources. This is a significant strength as it increases the accessibility and applicability of the research.
*   **Design-Based Research:**  The use of a design-based research approach is appropriate for exploring and refining the strategies for building the coding agent. The iterative cycles allow for a systematic investigation of different design choices.
*   **Controlled Experiments:** The controlled variable approach used for RQ1 and RQ2 is crucial for isolating the impact of data size on agent performance.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper systematically explores different approaches to improve the agent's performance, considering factors such as cognitive load, instruction structure, and feedback mechanisms.
*   **Detailed Strategies:** The paper articulates specific and actionable strategies for building customized GPT agents. This is a key contribution that can guide future research and practical applications.
*   **Transparent Methodology:** The paper clearly describes its methodology, data analysis techniques, and limitations, which promotes transparency and reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:** The study is based on data from UK primary schools and a specific coding scheme (CDAS). The generalizability of the findings to other contexts, subjects, or coding schemes is not fully established. The authors do acknowledge this.
*   **Reliance on Human-Annotated Data:** While the goal is automation, the study still relies on human-annotated data for training and evaluation. The accuracy of the agent is ultimately limited by the quality of these annotations. Further work is needed to explore methods for reducing this dependence.
*   **Subjective Performance Metric:** The performance metrics are based on agreement with human coders, which isn't necessarily indicative of absolute correctness. In dialogue coding there is not always a single correct code. Furthermore, inter-coder reliability is only reported as around 70-75%. As such, how far is the baseline and final outcome performing compared to *perfect* coding?
*   **Token Limits and Specific Tool:** The specific token limits and features of the MyGPT agent influence the strategies developed. The results may not directly translate to other LLM platforms with different architectures or capabilities.
*   **Incremental Novelty:** The application of existing techniques (like decision trees, structured prompts) to this specific context is helpful, but perhaps not ground-breaking.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in its focus on *practical* strategies for building customized GPT agents for dialogue analysis, particularly for researchers with limited resources. While other studies have explored LLMs for similar tasks, this paper offers a more accessible and replicable approach by focusing on a readily available tool (MyGPT) and developing clear, actionable guidelines.

The significance of the work stems from its potential to democratize access to automated dialogue analysis, enabling researchers and educators to analyze classroom interactions more efficiently and effectively. It is particularly relevant to fields like education, communication, and social sciences.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a practical need in dialogue analysis. The strategies proposed are well-reasoned, actionable, and tailored to the constraints of readily available LLMs. The paper's strengths lie in its practical focus, controlled experiments, and detailed recommendations. However, the study's limited generalizability, reliance on human-annotated data, the incremental nature of some of its findings, and the lack of comparison against *perfect* coding slightly reduce its overall score. The paper addresses a niche problem in an impactful and detailed manner. The replicability of its techniques make it a valuable piece of applied methodology.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### SAP-Bench: Benchmarking Multimodal Large Language Models in Surgical Action Planning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07196v1)
- **Authors**: Mengya Xu, Zhongzhen Huang, Dillan Imans, Yiru Ye, Xiaofan Zhang, Qi Dou
- **Abstract**: Effective evaluation is critical for driving advancements in MLLM research. The surgical action planning (SAP) task, which aims to generate future action sequences from visual inputs, demands precise and sophisticated analytical capabilities. Unlike mathematical reasoning, surgical decision-making operates in life-critical domains and requires meticulous, verifiable processes to ensure reliability and patient safety. This task demands the ability to distinguish between atomic visual actions and coordinate complex, long-horizon procedures, capabilities that are inadequately evaluated by current benchmarks. To address this gap, we introduce SAP-Bench, a large-scale, high-quality dataset designed to enable multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to perform interpretable surgical action planning. Our SAP-Bench benchmark, derived from the cholecystectomy procedures context with the mean duration of 1137.5s, and introduces temporally-grounded surgical action annotations, comprising the 1,226 clinically validated action clips (mean duration: 68.7s) capturing five fundamental surgical actions across 74 procedures. The dataset provides 1,152 strategically sampled current frames, each paired with the corresponding next action as multimodal analysis anchors. We propose the MLLM-SAP framework that leverages MLLMs to generate next action recommendations from the current surgical scene and natural language instructions, enhanced with injected surgical domain knowledge. To assess our dataset's effectiveness and the broader capabilities of current models, we evaluate seven state-of-the-art MLLMs (e.g., OpenAI-o1, GPT-4o, QwenVL2.5-72B, Claude-3.5-Sonnet, GeminiPro2.5, Step-1o, and GLM-4v) and reveal critical gaps in next action prediction performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "SAP-Bench," a new benchmark designed to evaluate multimodal large language models (MLLMs) in the context of surgical action planning.  SAP-Bench uses cholecystectomy (gallbladder removal) procedures, specifically focusing on the Calot's Triangle dissection phase. The dataset contains temporally-grounded annotations of surgical actions, including aspiration, coagulation, dissection, tissue retraction, and vessel clipping. It includes "current frames" as visual inputs, paired with the corresponding "next actions." The authors also propose the MLLM-SAP framework to generate next-action recommendations.  They tested several state-of-the-art MLLMs on the benchmark and identified performance gaps.  Furthermore, they incorporate expert surgeon feedback to establish clinical benchmarks and perform ablation studies on prompt engineering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of a surgical action planning benchmark.  While there are existing datasets for surgical AI (e.g., phase recognition, instrument segmentation), this paper focuses specifically on *proactive* action planning, combining visual understanding with procedural knowledge to predict future steps. This is a more complex and clinically relevant task than most previous benchmarks in the domain. The use of expert surgeon feedback to refine the ground truth represents a valuable addition, acknowledging the inherent variability in surgical procedures.
    However, The framework introduces a novel "surgical action planning task," where the goal is to generate future surgical action plans based on visual inputs. This task involves understanding atomic tool-tissue interactions and reasoning across long horizons. The problem is compelling and a nice contrast to "static" tasks in the medical space, which may offer a strong motivation for further work.

*   **Significance:**  Surgical action planning is a high-stakes, clinically important problem. A benchmark like SAP-Bench can potentially drive advancements in AI-assisted surgery, including intraoperative decision support and procedural automation. The paper's findings highlight limitations in current MLLMs' ability to perform this task, motivating further research in this direction.  The release of the dataset itself is a significant contribution, enabling others to explore and improve MLLM performance in surgical contexts.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The dataset is well-defined and annotated with clinically relevant actions.
    *   The use of real surgical video provides a realistic evaluation setting.
    *   The inclusion of expert surgeon feedback adds robustness to the evaluation.
    *   The MLLM-SAP framework provides a starting point for future research.
    *   Ablation studies offer insights into the impact of different prompt engineering components.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The scope is limited to cholecystectomy procedures. While a good starting point, broader generalizability across different surgical specialties should be considered.
    *   The MLLM-SAP framework, while sensible, is relatively straightforward. More sophisticated reasoning architectures (e.g., incorporating Chain-of-Thought reasoning more formally) could be explored.
    *   The evaluation focuses primarily on accuracy metrics (Sample-S, Video-S, Sample-R, Video-R).  Additional metrics related to safety (e.g., the potential for adverse events) would further enhance the clinical relevance of the benchmark.
    *   The ablation study, while providing some insights, can be expanded to explore the effectiveness of the "surgical knowledge injection" (via system prompt) in greater depth.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of MLLM research in surgical AI. The benchmark provides a valuable resource for the community, and the findings highlight areas where current models need improvement. The incorporation of expert surgeon feedback adds a layer of clinical validity that is often missing in AI research. Future studies can build upon SAP-Bench to develop more sophisticated surgical action planning systems.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper introduces a novel and clinically relevant benchmark that can significantly advance MLLM research in surgical AI.  The dataset and framework are well-defined and the experiments are thorough. However, the scope is limited to one type of surgery, the MLLM-SAP framework is relatively basic, and the evaluation metrics could be expanded to include safety-related measures. Despite these limitations, the paper is a valuable contribution to the field and warrants a score of 7. The potential to influence the development of AI-assisted surgical tools makes it a significant contribution deserving of recognition and attention.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reasoning Multimodal Large Language Model: Data Contamination and Dynamic Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07202v1)
- **Authors**: Ming Liu, Wensheng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) show impressive vision-language benchmark performance, yet growing concerns about data contamination (test set exposure during training) risk masking true generalization. This concern extends to reasoning MLLMs, often fine-tuned via reinforcement learning from potentially contaminated base models. We propose a novel dynamic evaluation framework to rigorously assess MLLM generalization, moving beyond static benchmarks. Instead of perturbing inputs, we perturb the task itself. Using the same visual input, models are evaluated across a family of tasks (e.g., QA, captioning, question posing, verification) to probe diverse capabilities. This task perturbation reveals whether model performance is robust or reliant on superficial task-specific cues. Our approach is analogous to loss landscape sharpness: models overfit or contaminated for a single task (sharp minima) falter under task shifts, unlike models with generalizable solutions (flatter minima). We developed an automated pipeline with a calibrated judge scoring open-ended generations (captions, questions) using paraphrase and corruption sampling. Applying this framework to leading image/video MLLMs on benchmarks including MME, RealWorldQA, and CVRR-ES, we analyze each model's cross-task "ability vector." We demonstrate that fine-tuning on simulated test data (extreme contamination) drastically sharpens task-specific performance but harms overall generalization. Our dynamic task perturbation offers deeper insights into MLLM generalization, distinguishing genuine understanding from spurious leakage or overfitting.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the growing concern of data contamination in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), where models may achieve high benchmark scores by memorizing test examples rather than demonstrating genuine generalization. The authors propose a novel dynamic evaluation framework that perturbs the *task* itself, rather than the input.  This involves evaluating MLLMs on a family of related tasks (QA, captioning, question generation, answer verification) for the same visual input.  The rationale is that models overfitting or contaminated for a single task will show a significant performance drop when shifted to other related tasks, unlike models with more generalizable understanding. The framework includes an automated pipeline and a calibrated "judge" model to score open-ended generations. Experiments on various benchmarks analyze each model's cross-task "ability vector," revealing how fine-tuning on simulated contaminated data significantly sharpens task-specific performance but harms overall generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of perturbing the *task* instead of the input is the paper's key contribution. This approach offers a fresh perspective on dynamic evaluation, moving beyond existing methods that focus on input variations, which often suffer from limitations (minor changes being ineffective, large changes altering the task's semantic intent). The concept of "task-space sharpness" as an indicator of overfitting or contamination is also a novel contribution, providing a theoretical framework for understanding the observed performance variations.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical issue in MLLM evaluation – the unreliability of static benchmarks due to data contamination. The proposed framework offers a more rigorous and informative way to assess the true generalization ability of MLLMs. Demonstrating how fine-tuning on contaminated data impacts cross-task performance provides concrete evidence of the limitations of relying solely on standard benchmark scores. The findings encourage the development of models that learn genuine visual understanding instead of exploiting spurious correlations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined framework:**  The methodology is clearly explained, including the task family design, automated evaluation pipeline, and metrics for assessing robustness.
    *   **Theoretical grounding:**  The connection to loss landscape sharpness provides a solid theoretical basis for the approach, explaining why task perturbation reveals contamination.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The framework is applied to a diverse set of MLLMs and benchmarks (image and video), providing broad insights into model behavior.
    *   **Controlled experiment:**  The contamination simulation (fine-tuning on test data) effectively demonstrates the framework's ability to detect overfitting.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Judge model bias:** The automated evaluation of generative tasks relies on a reasoning MLLM as a "judge."  This introduces a potential for bias based on the judge model's own training data and biases. While the authors calibrate the judge, some residual bias likely remains, influencing the scoring of captions and questions.
    *   **Task family limitations:** The four-task family, while well-chosen, may not capture all aspects of MLLM reasoning abilities. Expanding the task repertoire (e.g., including more complex reasoning tasks, explanation generation) could provide an even more comprehensive assessment.
    *   **Manual task augmentation:** The process for generating different types of tasks is currently manual. Automating this would allow testing with a greater number of different task types.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's framework has the potential to become a standard methodology for evaluating MLLM generalization ability. It encourages the development of evaluation metrics that go beyond simple accuracy scores, emphasizing robustness and cross-task consistency. The work also highlights the importance of considering data contamination when interpreting benchmark results, potentially influencing how future models are trained and evaluated.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper offers a novel and well-executed approach to dynamic MLLM evaluation, directly addressing a critical limitation of existing benchmarks. The task perturbation framework and the associated theoretical analysis (task-space sharpness) are valuable contributions. The comprehensive experiments and controlled contamination simulation demonstrate the effectiveness of the method.
