### MalGEN: A Generative Agent Framework for Modeling Malicious Software in Cybersecurity
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07586v1)
- **Authors**: Bikash Saha, Sandeep Kumar Shukla
- **Abstract**: The dual use nature of Large Language Models (LLMs) presents a growing challenge in cybersecurity. While LLM enhances automation and reasoning for defenders, they also introduce new risks, particularly their potential to be misused for generating evasive, AI crafted malware. Despite this emerging threat, the research community currently lacks controlled and extensible tools that can simulate such behavior for testing and defense preparation. We present MalGEN, a multi agent framework that simulates coordinated adversarial behavior to generate diverse, activity driven malware samples. The agents work collaboratively to emulate attacker workflows, including payload planning, capability selection, and evasion strategies, within a controlled environment built for ethical and defensive research. Using MalGEN, we synthesized ten novel malware samples and evaluated them against leading antivirus and behavioral detection engines. Several samples exhibited stealthy and evasive characteristics that bypassed current defenses, validating MalGEN's ability to model sophisticated and new threats. By transforming the threat of LLM misuse into an opportunity for proactive defense, MalGEN offers a valuable framework for evaluating and strengthening cybersecurity systems. The framework addresses data scarcity, enables rigorous testing, and supports the development of resilient and future ready detection strategies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MalGEN, a novel multi-agent framework that simulates the generation of malicious software using Large Language Models (LLMs).  The framework is designed for defensive cybersecurity research, allowing security professionals to proactively test and strengthen their systems against potential misuse of LLMs by attackers.  MalGEN utilizes a multi-agent architecture where each agent is responsible for a specific stage in the attack chain (e.g., planning, payload generation, evasion).  The framework generates diverse, activity-driven malware samples aligned with realistic threat scenarios and maps them to MITRE ATT&CK techniques.  The authors validate MalGEN by generating several malware samples and evaluating them against antivirus and behavioral detection engines, demonstrating the ability to evade some current defenses. The work aims to address the scarcity of tools for simulating AI-generated malware and to promote ethical red teaming.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its **holistic approach** to malware generation using LLMs. While other research explores LLM code generation or static analysis, MalGEN offers a **multi-agent framework** that simulates a coordinated adversarial workflow. Mapping generated malware to the MITRE ATT&CK framework also helps to benchmark the malicious behavior.  This contrasts with existing approaches that often rely on hand-crafted samples or static datasets. The design choices such as usage of compact LLMs and a clear separation of agents are key to promote better interpretability. The ability to generate realistic scenarios from user queries and map the actions to known attack campaigns is also valuable.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant and emerging problem in cybersecurity. The dual-use nature of LLMs poses a genuine threat, and the ability to simulate and understand AI-driven attacks is crucial for developing robust defenses. MalGEN provides a valuable tool for researchers, red teams, and security operations teams to prepare for this threat. In addition, the paper takes an important step by formalizing an ethical approach to malware simulation which is critical for these types of experiments.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined architecture:** The multi-agent design promotes modularity, scalability, and interpretability.
    *   **Realistic threat simulation:** The framework generates behaviorally diverse malware samples that mimic real-world TTPs.
    *   **Ethical safeguards:** MalGEN operates in a controlled environment, and artifacts are intended solely for defensive research.
    *   **Clear evaluation:** The authors provide a comprehensive evaluation with quantitative results and qualitative examples.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Language Limitation:** The framework currently only supports Python, which might limit the realism of generated malware compared to malware written in lower-level or other languages.
    *   **Lack of Validation Mechanism:** The generated code lacks validation and thus might fail to perform expected actions.
    *   **Limited Scope:**  The framework avoids generating highly destructive malware behaviors, thus limiting its use for simulating certain threat scenarios.
    *   **Static analysis focus:** The current evaluation heavily relies on VirusTotal's analysis, which focuses on static detection. A more rigorous dynamic analysis and deeper examination of the generated code's behavior in a sandbox environment would strengthen the evaluation.

*   **Potential Influence:**  MalGEN can serve as a valuable research platform for:
    *   Developing and evaluating AI-powered malware detection systems.
    *   Training security analysts on AI-driven attacks.
    *   Creating realistic red team scenarios.
    *   Building datasets for behavior-aware threat modeling.

**Justification:**

While MalGEN exhibits limitations (e.g., Python-only, limited dynamic analysis), its contribution to addressing the emerging threat of AI-generated malware is significant. The multi-agent framework, ethical safeguards, and ability to generate behaviorally diverse samples make it a valuable tool for defensive cybersecurity research. The framework's modularity allows for future extensions and improvements.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Explore the vulnerability of black-box models via diffusion models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07590v1)
- **Authors**: Jiacheng Shi, Yanfu Zhang, Huajie Shao, Ashley Gao
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in diffusion models have enabled high-fidelity and photorealistic image generation across diverse applications. However, these models also present security and privacy risks, including copyright violations, sensitive information leakage, and the creation of harmful or offensive content that could be exploited maliciously. In this study, we uncover a novel security threat where an attacker leverages diffusion model APIs to generate synthetic images, which are then used to train a high-performing substitute model. This enables the attacker to execute model extraction and transfer-based adversarial attacks on black-box classification models with minimal queries, without needing access to the original training data. The generated images are sufficiently high-resolution and diverse to train a substitute model whose outputs closely match those of the target model. Across the seven benchmarks, including CIFAR and ImageNet subsets, our method shows an average improvement of 27.37% over state-of-the-art methods while using just 0.01 times of the query budget, achieving a 98.68% success rate in adversarial attacks on the target model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the vulnerability of black-box machine learning models to model extraction and adversarial transfer attacks by leveraging diffusion model APIs. The authors propose a method where an attacker uses diffusion model APIs (like Stable Diffusion or DALL-E) to generate synthetic images. These images are then used to train a substitute model, which is subsequently used to perform model extraction and launch adversarial transfer attacks on the targeted black-box model. The method requires minimal queries to the target model and doesn't need access to the original training data. The experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach across several benchmark datasets, showing significant improvements over existing methods in terms of attack success rate and query efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using diffusion models as a source of training data for substitute models in black-box attacks is potentially novel, especially its focus on reducing query complexity by pretraining on the API generated data. Existing model extraction methods relied heavily on target model querying to create synthetic data and therefore did not scale well. The integration of freely available diffusion APIs is a pragmatic and relevant contribution. Prior work touched on using diffusion models for transfer attacks but did not emphasize the query-efficiency aspect as strongly as this paper does.

*   **Significance:** The work highlights an emerging security risk enabled by the increasing availability and sophistication of diffusion models. By demonstrating that diffusion models can be leveraged to train substitute models with minimal interaction with the target, the paper provides a practical illustration of this threat and suggests the potential for real-world attacks. The query-efficiency is particularly important, as it lowers the barrier to entry for attackers and makes it difficult for black-box systems to defend themselves. The paper's findings could motivate further research into defense mechanisms against this type of attack, such as input validation, rate limiting, and anomaly detection.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical relevance:**  The exploitation of widely accessible diffusion APIs adds a realistic dimension to the attack scenario, making the research practically relevant and timely.
    *   **Query efficiency:** A significant emphasis is placed on minimizing the number of queries to the target model. This focus makes the attack more stealthy and efficient.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The experimental results show convincing improvements over existing methods across several benchmark datasets.
    *   **Thorough ablation studies:** The paper includes detailed ablation studies that analyze the impact of pre-training and the selection of substitute model architectures, providing valuable insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited defense strategies:** While the paper focuses on the attack, it lacks a detailed discussion of potential defense mechanisms. Although identifying the vulnerability is important, discussion of potential mitigation approaches would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Simplistic prompt engineering:** The use of class-level prompts like "a photo of a [class]" might be considered somewhat basic. Exploring more complex and diverse prompts could potentially yield even better results, but the current approach highlights the efficacy using simpler more general approaches.

*   **Overall Impact:** The paper's findings are relevant to both the security and machine learning communities. The practical demonstration of an effective black-box attack using diffusion models helps raise awareness of this growing threat. This could initiate further investigations into the potential risks associated with publicly available generative models and spur the development of effective defenses.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper demonstrates solid novelty and significance by showcasing the use of diffusion models to launch effective model extraction and adversarial transfer attacks with minimal target model querying. While the evaluation of potential defense strategies remains limited and there are opportunities for expanding prompt engineering and diversification, the query-efficient attack methodology is noteworthy. The experiments exhibit tangible advantages in comparison to existing methodologies, demonstrating its significance within the field. The paper's weaknesses do not undermine its primary conclusion, but rather suggest directions for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating LLMs Effectiveness in Detecting and Correcting Test Smells: An Empirical Study
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07594v1)
- **Authors**: E. G. Santana Jr, Jander Pereira Santos Junior, Erlon P. Almeida, Iftekhar Ahmed, Paulo Anselmo da Mota Silveira Neto, Eduardo Santana de Almeida
- **Abstract**: Test smells indicate poor development practices in test code, reducing maintainability and reliability. While developers often struggle to prevent or refactor these issues, existing tools focus primarily on detection rather than automated refactoring. Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong potential in code understanding and transformation, but their ability to both identify and refactor test smells remains underexplored. We evaluated GPT-4-Turbo, LLaMA 3 70B, and Gemini-1.5 Pro on Python and Java test suites, using PyNose and TsDetect for initial smell detection, followed by LLM-driven refactoring. Gemini achieved the highest detection accuracy (74.35\% Python, 80.32\% Java), while LLaMA was lowest. All models could refactor smells, but effectiveness varied, sometimes introducing new smells. Gemini also improved test coverage, unlike GPT-4 and LLaMA, which often reduced it. These results highlight LLMs' potential for automated test smell refactoring, with Gemini as the strongest performer, though challenges remain across languages and smell types.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) like GPT-4 Turbo, LLaMA 3 70B, and Gemini 1.5 Pro to detect and refactor test smells in Python and Java code. The authors use existing test smell detection tools (PyNose and TsDetect) to identify smells and then prompt the LLMs to refactor the code. They evaluate the LLMs based on detection accuracy, refactoring effectiveness (including the introduction of new smells), and the impact on test coverage. The results show that Gemini generally performs best in detection, while the refactoring performance varies across languages and smell types, with all models sometimes introducing new smells or negatively impacting coverage.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art LLMs specifically for *test smell detection and refactoring*, an area that, as the authors point out, has received relatively little attention compared to code generation or repair. While there's existing work on using LLMs for software testing tasks, this focuses the lens specifically on test maintainability and code quality improvements, which is a valuable contribution. The use of a hybrid approach, combining static analysis tools with LLM-based refactoring, is also a practical and novel aspect.
*   **Significance:** The study's significance stems from the potential to automate and improve the quality of test code. Test smells are a real problem in software development, and if LLMs can reliably detect and refactor them, this could lead to more maintainable, reliable, and less error-prone software. The study contributes valuable empirical evidence on the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in this domain. Understanding how LLMs can unintentionally introduce new smells or reduce coverage is particularly important.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper has a well-defined research question and a clear methodology, involving multiple LLMs, two programming languages, and a diverse set of test smells.
    *   The use of established test smell detection tools (PyNose and TsDetect) as oracles increases the reliability of the evaluation.
    *   The analysis of refactoring outcomes includes not only smell removal but also the introduction of new smells and the impact on test coverage, providing a comprehensive view.
    *   The prompt engineering process is described and the rationale for prompt selection is clearly explained.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While a few prompt engineering strategies were used, the space is vast. There could be prompts that produce much better results than those used in this work.
    *   The datasets used come from prior literature, while this is not a critical issue, it may be better to create a dataset specifically for test smells.
    *   The number of examples in test smell refactoring is rather limited. More examples would improve the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The reliance on coverage analysis as a sole indicator of refactoring impact has limitations.  A qualitative assessment of the resulting test code would add further value.
    *   The experiments are confined to open-source repositories. The behavior of LLMs might be different on proprietary or legacy codebases.
*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to stimulate further research in this area, guiding the development of more effective LLM-based tools for test code maintenance. The identified limitations and trade-offs will help researchers focus on improving the robustness and reliability of LLM-driven refactoring. The open availability of experimental artifacts supports reproducibility and further investigation.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by systematically evaluating LLMs for a relatively unexplored task (test smell management) and identifying both their strengths and weaknesses. The study provides valuable empirical evidence and practical insights that will inform future research and development. The limitations mentioned above, however, prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. While novel and significant, it's more of a careful exploration than a revolutionary breakthrough.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### TwinBreak: Jailbreaking LLM Security Alignments based on Twin Prompts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07596v1)
- **Authors**: Torsten Krauß, Hamid Dashtbani, Alexandra Dmitrienko
- **Abstract**: Machine learning is advancing rapidly, with applications bringing notable benefits, such as improvements in translation and code generation. Models like ChatGPT, powered by Large Language Models (LLMs), are increasingly integrated into daily life. However, alongside these benefits, LLMs also introduce social risks. Malicious users can exploit LLMs by submitting harmful prompts, such as requesting instructions for illegal activities. To mitigate this, models often include a security mechanism that automatically rejects such harmful prompts. However, they can be bypassed through LLM jailbreaks. Current jailbreaks often require significant manual effort, high computational costs, or result in excessive model modifications that may degrade regular utility. We introduce TwinBreak, an innovative safety alignment removal method. Building on the idea that the safety mechanism operates like an embedded backdoor, TwinBreak identifies and prunes parameters responsible for this functionality. By focusing on the most relevant model layers, TwinBreak performs fine-grained analysis of parameters essential to model utility and safety. TwinBreak is the first method to analyze intermediate outputs from prompts with high structural and content similarity to isolate safety parameters. We present the TwinPrompt dataset containing 100 such twin prompts. Experiments confirm TwinBreak's effectiveness, achieving 89% to 98% success rates with minimal computational requirements across 16 LLMs from five vendors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TwinBreak, a white-box method for jailbreaking Large Language Models (LLMs) by removing safety alignments. It identifies and prunes parameters responsible for safety mechanisms by analyzing activation differences between harmful and harmless "twin prompts" - prompts that are highly similar in structure and content. This targeted pruning minimizes utility loss compared to other methods. The paper presents a new dataset, TwinPrompt, of 100 such twin prompts. Experiments on 16 models from five vendors show high attack success rates (89-98%) with minimal computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its use of twin prompts for fine-grained analysis of safety mechanisms. The concept of safety as a backdoor is not entirely new, and pruning techniques exist. However, the twin prompt idea, combined with iterative targeted pruning focused on specific layers (Gate and Up), differentiates this work from existing black-box and white-box jailbreaking methods.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing the trade-off between safety alignment and model utility in LLMs. By selectively pruning, TwinBreak aims to remove safety features without significantly degrading model performance. The reported high success rates across diverse models, coupled with relatively low computational requirements, suggests a potentially practical method for red-teaming or understanding safety alignment weaknesses. It is also impactful since the approach is relatively independent of the model instance. The paper helps point out some weaknesses in the safety alignment mechanisms of recent LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Twin Prompt approach:** The core idea of using twin prompts to pinpoint safety parameters is innovative and offers a way to analyze subtle activation differences.
    *   **Targeted Pruning:** Focusing on specific layers (Gate, Up), reduces unnecessary modifications and helps preserve utility.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments across diverse LLMs and datasets demonstrate the effectiveness and generalizability of TwinBreak.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** The method requires modest computational resources and runtime, making it more practical than some other white-box approaches.
    *   The paper makes a contribution to the open LLM research community since the new dataset is to be released upon acceptance of the paper.
    *   Strong comparisons to existing work show that the approach leads to improvements in utility and safety alignment removal compared to methods such as those proposed by Wei et al.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dataset Dependence:** Although the method is model-independent, it still relies on the creation of a TwinPrompt dataset, which, while a one-time effort, requires manual crafting. The quality of the twin prompts would influence the results.
    *   **Utility Analysis:** While the authors claim minimal impact on utility, there is some degradation observed in utility benchmark results, however, the differences are subtle. The claim of minimal loss should be further scrutinized as real-world applications may be more sensitive to accuracy changes.
    *   **Defenses:** The paper offers limited discussion on potential defenses against TwinBreak. Though it mentions strengthening safety alignment by distributing it across more parameters, a more in-depth exploration would be beneficial.
    *   The ethical considerations section is very basic and does not address the risks or potential applications of such a jailbreak method when applied outside of red-teaming research.

*   **Potential Influence:** TwinBreak has the potential to influence the field by providing a more efficient and targeted approach to jailbreaking LLMs. It can be used for red-teaming activities, vulnerability assessment, and a better understanding of safety alignment mechanisms. The insights into the location of safety parameters could inform the development of more robust alignment techniques.

*   **Overall:**

The paper presents a compelling and well-supported method for removing safety alignments in LLMs. The twin-prompt approach is novel and effective. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses, and it offers a valuable contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a novel technique with promising results and addresses a critical problem in LLM security. It offers some clear advantages over other similar work and makes a contribution to the research community. A slightly higher score is not awarded due to the need for manual dataset creation, the potentially over-stated claim of "minimal" utility loss (needs closer analysis), and insufficient discussion of defenses and ethical considerations.
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Instructing Large Language Models for Low-Resource Languages: A Systematic Study for Basque
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07597v1)
- **Authors**: Oscar Sainz, Naiara Perez, Julen Etxaniz, Joseba Fernandez de Landa, Itziar Aldabe, Iker García-Ferrero, Aimar Zabala, Ekhi Azurmendi, German Rigau, Eneko Agirre, Mikel Artetxe, Aitor Soroa
- **Abstract**: Instructing language models with user intent requires large instruction datasets, which are only available for a limited set of languages. In this paper, we explore alternatives to conventional instruction adaptation pipelines in low-resource scenarios. We assume a realistic scenario for low-resource languages, where only the following are available: corpora in the target language, existing open-weight multilingual base and instructed backbone LLMs, and synthetically generated instructions sampled from the instructed backbone. We present a comprehensive set of experiments for Basque that systematically study different combinations of these components evaluated on benchmarks and human preferences from 1,680 participants. Our conclusions show that target language corpora are essential, with synthetic instructions yielding robust models, and, most importantly, that using as backbone an instruction-tuned model outperforms using a base non-instructed model, and improved results when scaling up. Using Llama 3.1 instruct 70B as backbone our model comes near frontier models of much larger sizes for Basque, without using any Basque data apart from the 1.2B word corpora. We release code, models, instruction datasets, and human preferences to support full reproducibility in future research on low-resource language adaptation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Instructing Large Language Models for Low-Resource Languages: A Systematic Study for Basque":

**Summary:**

This paper explores effective strategies for adapting Large Language Models (LLMs) to low-resource languages, focusing specifically on Basque. Faced with limited instruction data, the authors systematically investigate different combinations of target language corpora, open-weight multilingual base models, pre-trained instruction-tuned backbones, and synthetically generated instructions. They present a comprehensive experimental framework and evaluate their models using both static benchmarks and human preferences gathered from a large-scale community-driven evaluation effort. The key findings indicate that: 1) target language corpora are essential for good performance, 2) synthetic instructions work well, and 3) starting with a pre-trained instruction-tuned model outperforms beginning with a non-instructed base model.  Their best model, built on Llama 3.1 instruct 70B, achieves competitive performance for Basque, approaching the capabilities of much larger commercial models. The paper also releases their code, models, instruction datasets, and human preference data to facilitate further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's strength lies in its systematic and thorough exploration of different adaptation strategies for instruction-tuning LLMs in low-resource settings. While existing research addresses adapting LLMs for such languages, this work goes beyond a single approach and analyzes multiple combinations of resources (corpora, base models, synthetic data) and training methodologies. The deliberate constraint to readily available/creatable resources, avoiding reliance on commercial distillation, is both a strength (increased applicability) and a slight limitation (potential ceiling on performance).

* **Significance:** The insights gained are significant for researchers and practitioners working with low-resource languages. The finding that pre-trained instruction-tuned models provide a better starting point than base models challenges the standard adaptation pipeline and has implications for future research directions. The successful use of synthetic instructions is important, given the scarcity of manually-created instruction data. The use of human preferences for evaluation, and the large scale of the effort, is also notable and addresses a critical problem in evaluating LLMs, specifically for languages that are not that well supported.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Systematic approach:** The paper rigorously tests different combinations of resources and strategies, making the results and the conclusions more reliable.
    * **Realistic setup:** The constraints on resources (availability and createability) align with challenges faced in genuine low-resource scenarios.
    * **Large-scale human evaluation:** The community-driven arena, with over 12,000 annotations, is a significant contribution. It provides valuable insights into the perceived quality of models that static benchmarks may miss.
    * **Open Release:** The authors' commitment to releasing data, code, and models greatly benefits the research community.
    * **State of the Art Performance with open weight LLM**: The 70B model approaches state of the art in Basque, without using proprietary datasets, by only using a 1.2B word corpus.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Language Specificity:** While the findings likely generalize to other similarly-resourced languages, the study is focused on a single language (Basque). More explicit discussion of the nuances of Basque and how they might affect generalizability would be beneficial. The paper could benefit from adding more details on the limitations.
    * **Limited Scope of Alignment:**  The paper focuses only on the initial instruction tuning phase. While preference data was gathered, alignment tuning was not explored due to time and compute limitations, but should be considered part of the full adaptation pipeline.
    * **Missing ablations for 70B**: As said by the authors in page 7, one of the reasons why their model underperforms SotA is the weaker backbone model. More explanation regarding why INS EN model was chosen as a backbone would be of great value. The ablation studies focused on smaller model sizes. While practical, it leaves some questions open about how these insights translate to very large models.

* **Potential Influence:** This paper could significantly influence the direction of research on LLMs for low-resource languages.  It provides a practical guide for adapting models with limited resources, and the released datasets will likely become valuable assets for the community. The emphasis on human evaluation will also encourage researchers to adopt more user-centric evaluation strategies.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper is a significant contribution to the field. The systematic study, the emphasis on realistic constraints and available resources, the large-scale human evaluation, and the open release of data and code are all commendable strengths. The challenge to the standard adaptation pipeline and the success with synthetic data have the potential to influence the field. However, the language-specificity of the study and the lack of alignment tuning provide minor limitations. While the paper does provide solid insight for similar languages, a higher score requires more extensive validation and/or demonstration of applicability across a wider range of linguistic families. The analysis for the 70B would provide additional insight that a score above 8 requires. This paper also contributes significantly to addressing the AI divide, as the AI ecosystem is dominantly English-centric. The paper encourages further research in this direction. Overall, the score of 8 reflects the paper's valuable contribution to the understanding and development of LLMs for low-resource languages.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### SceneRAG: Scene-level Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Video Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07600v1)
- **Authors**: Nianbo Zeng, Haowen Hou, Fei Richard Yu, Si Shi, Ying Tiffany He
- **Abstract**: Despite recent advances in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) for video understanding, effectively understanding long-form video content remains underexplored due to the vast scale and high complexity of video data. Current RAG approaches typically segment videos into fixed-length chunks, which often disrupts the continuity of contextual information and fails to capture authentic scene boundaries. Inspired by the human ability to naturally organize continuous experiences into coherent scenes, we present SceneRAG, a unified framework that leverages large language models to segment videos into narrative-consistent scenes by processing ASR transcripts alongside temporal metadata. SceneRAG further sharpens these initial boundaries through lightweight heuristics and iterative correction. For each scene, the framework fuses information from both visual and textual modalities to extract entity relations and dynamically builds a knowledge graph, enabling robust multi-hop retrieval and generation that account for long-range dependencies. Experiments on the LongerVideos benchmark, featuring over 134 hours of diverse content, confirm that SceneRAG substantially outperforms prior baselines, achieving a win rate of up to 72.5 percent on generation tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the SceneRAG paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SceneRAG, a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework specifically designed for understanding long-form video content.  SceneRAG addresses limitations of traditional RAG approaches by segmenting videos into semantically coherent "scenes" using a large language model (LLM) that processes ASR transcripts and temporal metadata. This scene segmentation aims to better align with narrative flow.  The framework then constructs a lightweight knowledge graph for each scene, integrating visual and textual information to capture entity relations and long-range dependencies. This allows for multi-hop retrieval and context-rich generation.  The authors demonstrate significant improvements in generation tasks on the LongerVideos benchmark compared to existing RAG methods, achieving significantly high win-rates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the combination of several existing ideas into a cohesive and effective framework. The idea of scene segmentation for video is not new, nor is the use of knowledge graphs for RAG. However, the *specific* way these components are integrated—LLM-driven scene segmentation combined with a graph constructed from multimodal information within each scene for multi-hop retrieval—is a valuable contribution. The human-inspired scene segmentation is an interesting approach that could lead to more robust video understanding. The use of LLMs for dynamic segmentation, and the integration of visual and textual cues within each segmented scene for building a context-aware knowledge graph is also a strength.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is in addressing a crucial challenge: effectively processing and understanding long-form video.  Traditional RAG approaches struggle with the scale and complexity of video data, often disrupting context and failing to capture scene boundaries. SceneRAG provides a more principled approach, demonstrating substantial improvements in retrieval precision and generation quality. The LongerVideos dataset, containing 134+ hours of data, validates the effectiveness of the approach. The results show that SceneRAG consistently outperforms existing methods across various evaluation metrics. The significant performance improvement suggests potential benefits for downstream tasks such as video summarization, question answering, and video editing. The claim of a win rate up to 72.5 percent on generation tasks compared to baseline suggests its effectiveness.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a significant problem in video understanding.
    *   Combines existing techniques in a novel and effective way.
    *   Demonstrates substantial improvements on a large, realistic dataset.
    *   Provides a well-defined framework with clear components.
    *   Human-inspired segmentation has strong reasoning.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on ASR transcripts can be a limitation in scenarios with poor audio quality or lack of transcripts (Although the paper tries to remediate this via visual features).
    *   The computational cost of LLM-driven segmentation could be a barrier to wider adoption. The paper mentions using "faster-distil-whisper-large-v3", but the prompt engineering and multiple LLM calls likely add overhead.
    *   The evaluation primarily relies on LLM-based metrics (pairwise win-rate comparisons and Likert scale ratings against a baseline). While human-centric dimensions are used (comprehensiveness, helpfulness, etc.) and are a positive, more traditional metrics (e.g., accuracy of question answering) would further strengthen the results.
    *   The use of heuristics, while improving performance, introduce potential bias.

*   **Potential Influence:**  SceneRAG has the potential to influence future research in video understanding by demonstrating the importance of scene-aware processing and multimodal knowledge integration. The framework is well-defined and easily adaptable for addressing other video-related tasks. The findings may encourage exploration of incorporating cognitive segmentation principles for automated video indexing and retrieval. The framework could be expanded for video editing, summarization, and intelligent assistance applications.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:**
    SceneRAG is a valuable contribution to the field of video understanding. While not groundbreaking in its individual components, its carefully designed integration of LLM-driven scene segmentation, multimodal knowledge graphs, and multi-hop retrieval addresses a significant problem and demonstrates impressive performance improvements. The reliance on ASR transcripts and LLM-based evaluation metrics are limitations, and the novelty, while present, isn't fundamentally revolutionary. However, the solid experimental results, well-defined approach, and clear articulation of the problem make this paper worthy.

### PolitiSky24: U.S. Political Bluesky Dataset with User Stance Labels
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07606v1)
- **Authors**: Peyman Rostami, Vahid Rahimzadeh, Ali Adibi, Azadeh Shakery
- **Abstract**: Stance detection identifies the viewpoint expressed in text toward a specific target, such as a political figure. While previous datasets have focused primarily on tweet-level stances from established platforms, user-level stance resources, especially on emerging platforms like Bluesky remain scarce. User-level stance detection provides a more holistic view by considering a user's complete posting history rather than isolated posts. We present the first stance detection dataset for the 2024 U.S. presidential election, collected from Bluesky and centered on Kamala Harris and Donald Trump. The dataset comprises 16,044 user-target stance pairs enriched with engagement metadata, interaction graphs, and user posting histories. PolitiSky24 was created using a carefully evaluated pipeline combining advanced information retrieval and large language models, which generates stance labels with supporting rationales and text spans for transparency. The labeling approach achieves 81\% accuracy with scalable LLMs. This resource addresses gaps in political stance analysis through its timeliness, open-data nature, and user-level perspective. The dataset is available at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.15616911
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces POLITISKY24, a new dataset for stance detection focused on the 2024 U.S. presidential election, specifically targeting Kamala Harris and Donald Trump.  The dataset is collected from the Bluesky social network and contains user-level stances, enriched with engagement metadata, interaction graphs, and user posting histories.  The authors describe their pipeline for creating the dataset, which involves filtering users, retrieving relevant posts using embedding models, and labeling stance using large language models (LLMs). The paper details the data collection process, the stance labeling pipeline, and provides an analysis of LLM performance and error patterns. The authors evaluate several embedding models for context retrieval and LLMs for stance labeling, ultimately selecting KaLM-mini-v1.5 and DeepSeek-Chat-v3, respectively.  The paper concludes with an analysis of the stance distribution within the dataset and a discussion of potential ethical considerations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the dataset itself.  Existing stance detection datasets are primarily focused on Twitter/X and often concentrate on post-level analysis. The user-level perspective and the focus on the 2024 U.S. election using the emerging Bluesky platform are unique. The authors adequately motivate the need for datasets on alternative platforms due to API restrictions and centralized nature of traditional platforms like X. This addresses a significant gap in the field.

*   **Significance:** Stance detection is a crucial task in NLP, with applications in fake news detection, user profiling, and understanding public opinion. A dataset focusing on user-level stance on an emerging platform, particularly during a significant election year, contributes to this field. The effort to select relevant user-posts before using LLMs enhances the quality of labeling, and provides valuable insights. The inclusion of rationales and text spans helps with interpretability and error analysis. However, its impact depends on how widely and effectively the dataset is used by other researchers.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Dataset Creation:**  The paper presents a clear and detailed methodology for dataset creation, including user filtering, data collection, stance labeling, and evaluation.
    *   **Use of LLMs and Evaluation:**  The paper leverages the strengths of LLMs for stance detection while also providing a rigorous evaluation of their performance. The error analysis offers valuable insights into the challenges of stance detection.
    *   **User-Level Focus:**  The focus on user-level stance provides a more holistic perspective compared to tweet-level analysis.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** Explicit acknowledgment and discussion of the ethical aspects are commendable.
    *   **Open Data:** Making the dataset publicly available enhances reproducibility and fosters further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Potential Sampling Bias:** As the authors acknowledged, the reliance on hashtag-based user selection may introduce sampling bias. Users who actively use political hashtags might not be representative of the entire Bluesky population.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of Generalizability:**  The paper focuses heavily on the validation set. While important for pipeline configuration, there is a lack of a more comprehensive analysis of model generalizability to *new* users.
    *   **Bluesky Specificity:** The dataset is tied to the specific characteristics of the Bluesky platform.  While this is a strength in terms of novelty, it also limits the direct transferability of models trained on this dataset to other platforms with different user demographics and interaction styles.
    *   **English Language Constraint:** Limiting the analysis to English language only is restrictive. The platform may have a more global presence, so multilingual perspectives could add substantial richness to the analysis.

*   **Potential Influence:**  POLITISKY24 has the potential to stimulate research in stance detection, particularly on emerging social networks and user-level analysis. It also provides a valuable resource for analyzing public opinion during the 2024 U.S. election. The detailed methodology can serve as a blueprint for creating similar datasets in other domains. However, the dataset's utility is dependent on its adoption within the research community.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a novel and timely dataset that addresses a gap in the stance detection research field. The use of emerging platforms like Bluesky is essential in a rapidly changing technological landscape. The careful pipeline and comprehensive evaluation with LLMs shows substantial methodological effort. However, the inherent sampling bias and lack of generalizability analysis do temper the strength of this contribution. Despite these drawbacks, the paper offers a valuable resource and insights, thus warranting a score of:

- **Score**: 7/10

### Vuyko Mistral: Adapting LLMs for Low-Resource Dialectal Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07617v1)
- **Authors**: Roman Kyslyi, Yuliia Maksymiuk, Ihor Pysmennyi
- **Abstract**: In this paper we introduce the first effort to adapt large language models (LLMs) to the Ukrainian dialect (in our case Hutsul), a low-resource and morphologically complex dialect spoken in the Carpathian Highlands. We created a parallel corpus of 9852 dialect-to-standard Ukrainian sentence pairs and a dictionary of 7320 dialectal word mappings. We also addressed data shortage by proposing an advanced Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipeline to generate synthetic parallel translation pairs, expanding the corpus with 52142 examples. We have fine-tuned multiple open-source LLMs using LoRA and evaluated them on a standard-to-dialect translation task, also comparing with few-shot GPT-4o translation. In the absence of human annotators, we adopt a multi-metric evaluation strategy combining BLEU, chrF++, TER, and LLM-based judgment (GPT-4o). The results show that even small(7B) finetuned models outperform zero-shot baselines such as GPT-4o across both automatic and LLM-evaluated metrics. All data, models, and code are publicly released at: https://github.com/woters/vuyko-hutsul
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary, critical evaluation, and novelty score for the paper "Vuyko Mistral: Adapting LLMs for Low-Resource Dialectal Translation."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of adapting large language models (LLMs) to low-resource dialects, specifically the Hutsul dialect of Ukrainian.  The authors create a parallel corpus of Hutsul-Ukrainian sentence pairs, a Hutsul-Ukrainian dictionary, and augment the dataset using an advanced Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipeline to generate synthetic translation pairs. They fine-tune several open-source LLMs using LoRA and evaluate their performance on a standard-to-dialect translation task, comparing it with few-shot GPT-4o translation. They adopt a multi-metric evaluation strategy combining BLEU, chrF++, TER, and LLM-based judgment.  The results indicate that even smaller fine-tuned models outperform zero-shot baselines, including GPT-4o. The datasets, models, and code are made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in the specific application of LLMs to a low-resource Ukrainian dialect (Hutsul) and the comprehensive approach to data creation and model adaptation. While the techniques used (fine-tuning, LoRA, RAG) are not individually novel, their combination and application to this specific problem, particularly in the context of Ukrainian dialects, contribute to a novel approach. The creation of a parallel corpus, dictionary, and synthetic data generation pipeline, specifically tailored to the Hutsul dialect, is a unique contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in NLP research: the lack of attention to low-resource dialects. By adapting LLMs to the Hutsul dialect, the authors contribute to the preservation and accessibility of this important aspect of Ukrainian linguistic and cultural heritage. Furthermore, the data augmentation strategy using RAG could be valuable for other low-resource dialect adaptation tasks. The open release of the data, models, and code is a significant contribution, allowing other researchers to build upon their work.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Data Creation:**  The paper clearly outlines the process of creating the parallel corpus, dictionary, and synthetic data.  The RAG approach is interesting and well-explained. The details about cleaning and filtering the data are valuable.
    *   **Practical Application:**  The paper goes beyond theoretical exploration by fine-tuning several open-source LLMs and making them publicly available.
    *   **Multi-Metric Evaluation:** The use of both automatic metrics and LLM-based judgment provides a more robust and nuanced evaluation of the translation quality.  The justification for using LLM-based evaluation, acknowledging the limitations of standard metrics for dialectal translation, is well-argued.
    *   **Clear Presentation:**  The paper is well-structured, easy to follow, and provides sufficient details about the methodology and results.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Dialectal Coverage:** The study focuses on a single Ukrainian dialect.  While this allows for in-depth exploration, it limits the generalizability of the findings to other dialects or languages.
    *   **Synthetic Data Limitations:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of the synthetic data, particularly its lack of coverage of modern topics.  While the filtering process mitigates some of these issues, it still represents a potential source of bias.
    *   **GPT-4o as Evaluator:** While LLM based evaluation helps, GPT-4o's judgment may not be perfect, and could be biased based on the prompt it receives and/or a leaning toward standard Ukrainian, especially since it hasn't been explicitly trained on Hutsul. Human evaluation, though difficult to obtain, would have strengthened the results.
    *   **Baseline Performance:** While the paper mentions that Mistral or LLaMA models as baselines had much worse performance, it would have been useful to see these numbers, even if very low, for comparison.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in low-resource dialect adaptation. The data creation methodology, the model fine-tuning approach, and the evaluation strategy can serve as a valuable template for other researchers. The open release of resources will further facilitate progress in this area. The paper also highlights the importance of addressing the data scarcity and evaluation challenges in dialectal NLP.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel application of LLMs to a relevant problem (low-resource dialect translation) with a comprehensive approach to data creation and model adaptation. The work is thorough, well-evaluated, and includes a valuable contribution in releasing code and data. The limitations in the synthetic data and GPT-4o evaluation (compared to human evaluation) prevent a higher score. A more generalizable study would also have improved the score. Despite these limitations, the paper provides a solid foundation for future research in dialectal NLP and offers valuable insights into the challenges and opportunities of adapting LLMs to under-represented languages and dialects. The creation of specific datasets is very beneficial for future work. Therefore, I believe that a score of 7 is appropriate, reflecting the novelty, significance, and practical value of the work, while also acknowledging its limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### LoRMA: Low-Rank Multiplicative Adaptation for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.07621v1)
- **Authors**: Harsh Bihany, Shubham Patel, Ashutosh Modi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models have shown remarkable capabilities in the NLP domain. Their effectiveness can mainly be attributed to their ability to adapt to an array of downstream tasks. However, generally, full fine-tuning is a computationally expensive job. To mitigate this, many techniques have been developed that prime efficiency, a prominent one being Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). However, LoRA and its variants employ re-parametrized additive updates. In this paper, we propose Low-Rank Multiplicative Adaptation (LoRMA), which shifts the paradigm of additive updates to a richer space of matrix multiplicative transformations. We tackle challenges such as computational complexity and rank bottleneck of matrix multiplication by effectively re-ordering operations and introducing rank inflation strategies. We conduct extensive experiments to demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach in terms of various evaluation metrics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LoRMA (Low-Rank Multiplicative Adaptation), a novel parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) technique for large language models (LLMs). Unlike LoRA, which uses additive updates to the weight matrices of LLMs, LoRMA proposes multiplicative updates.  The paper addresses challenges associated with multiplicative updates, namely increased computational complexity and rank limitations due to matrix multiplication properties. It tackles these issues through strategic re-ordering of matrix operations and by introducing two rank inflation strategies: additive rank inflation (LoRMA+) and permutation-based rank inflation (LoRMAπ).  The authors conduct extensive experiments across various NLU and NLG tasks using transformer-based LLMs like RoBERTa, GPT-2, Gemma-2B, and LLaMA-3-8B, comparing LoRMA to LoRA and other PEFT baselines. The results indicate that LoRMA often achieves better performance and faster convergence than LoRA.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in shifting from additive to multiplicative low-rank adaptation. While LoRA and its variants have explored numerous optimizations around additive updates, LoRMA ventures into a fundamentally different update mechanism. The rank inflation strategies (additive and permutation-based) are crucial to overcoming the inherent rank limitations introduced by the multiplicative approach and showcase innovative solutions.  However, the high-level idea of multiplicative updates in neural networks is not entirely new. The effectiveness of the re-ordering and inflation techniques in overcoming rank bottlenecks is valuable.
*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate.  PEFT techniques are crucial for adapting LLMs to downstream tasks without incurring the full computational cost of fine-tuning. LoRMA potentially offers a more expressive way to modify weight matrices. If further research confirms its consistent superiority or distinct advantages (e.g., specific task suitability, better robustness), LoRMA could become a valuable addition to the PEFT toolkit. Demonstrating faster convergence is a significant advantage.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the motivation and technical details of LoRMA.
    *   The experimental setup is comprehensive, covering a diverse set of tasks and models.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components of LoRMA (rank inflation, matrix ordering).
    *   The analysis of weight update characteristics helps in understanding how LoRMA differs from LoRA.
    *   Faster convergence rate compared to LoRA.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The mathematical proof of the existence of a multiplicative transformation while interesting, its actual relevance to LLMs is marginal. A stronger connection between the proof and why multiplications are better in LLMs will be more persuasive.
    *   The computational complexity analysis needs better clarity.
    *   While the empirical results are promising, the improvements over LoRA are often marginal or task-dependent.  The reported AUC decreases might not always translate to significant real-world performance gains.
    *   The rank analysis, showing "almost full rank," needs to be quantified more precisely (e.g., by showing the distribution of singular values). This would better support the claim of LoRMA having a "richer update."
    *   The "high correlation" results could be interpreted as LoRMA essentially mimicking LoRA's behavior, raising questions about its distinctiveness. A deeper analysis of where LoRMA *diverges* from LoRA in weight updates, and how that affects performance, would be more valuable.
    *   Need to better motivate why the initial weights were nearly full rank.
    *   More detailed complexity explanation on how LORMA can be comparable to LORA.

*   **Potential Influence:** LoRMA could inspire further research into multiplicative adaptation techniques, potentially leading to novel PEFT methods that combine additive and multiplicative approaches.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a novel idea with a thorough experimental analysis. However, the improvements over LoRA, while present, are not consistently transformative and are somewhat task-dependent. While the paper presents reasonable performance compared to the many alternatives, the gains can be incremental, and it is unclear if this will continue with the larger models. The limitations should be studied further to gain a deeper understanding of when and why LoRMA works well. The weaknesses, especially those related to the magnitude of performance improvements and the clear demonstration of distinct advantages, prevent a higher score.
